A categorical characterization of relative entropy on standard Borel spaces Thanks: This research has been supported by NSERC and by Google.
Nicolas Gagné ORCID:0009-0004-0207-0106, Prakash Panangaden ORCID:0000-0001-8763-6172

Abstract
. We give a categorical treatment, in the spirit of Baez and Fritz, of relative entropy for probability distributions defined on standard Borel spaces. We define a category called 𝐒𝐛𝐒𝐭𝐚𝐭 {\mathbf{SbStat}} suitable for reasoning about statistical inference on standard Borel spaces. We define relative entropy as a functor into Lawvere’s category [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] and we show convexity, lower semicontinuity and uniqueness.



Jun. 25, 2021Nov. 09, 2023

\titlecomment
\lsuper

*An extended abstract of this work appeared in the

33^{rd}
Mathematical Foundations of Programming Semantics Conference.

[a] [b]

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 19, Issue 4, Paper 10

1.
Introduction

The inspiration for the present work comes from two recent developments.
The first is the beginning of a categorical understanding of Bayesian
inversion and learning
[
DG15
,
DDG16
,
CDDG17
,
DSDG18
]
. The second is a
categorical reconstruction of relative
entropy
[
BFL11
,
BF14
,
Lei
]
. The present paper provides a
categorical treatment of entropy in the spirit of Baez and Fritz in the
setting of standard Borel spaces, thus setting the stage to explore the role of
entropy in learning.

Recently there have been some exciting developments that bring some
categorical insights to probability theory and specifically to learning
theory. These are reported in some recent papers by Clerc, Dahlqvist,
Danos and Garnier
[
DG15
,
DDG16
,
CDDG17
]
. The first of these
papers showed how to view the Dirichlet distribution as a natural
transformation thus opening the way to an understanding of higher-order
probabilities, while the second gave a powerful framework for constructing
several natural transformations. In
[
DG15
]
the hope was expressed
that one could use these ideas to understand Bayesian inversion, a core
concept in machine learning. In
[
CDDG17
]
this was realized in a
remarkably novel way. These papers carry out their investigations in the
setting of standard Borel spaces and are based on the Giry
monad
[
Gir81
,
Law64
]
.

In
[
BFL11
,
BF14
]
a beautiful treatment of relative entropy is
given in categorical terms. The basic idea is to understand entropy in
terms of the results of experiments and observations. How much does one
learn about a probabilistic situation by doing experiments and observing
the results? A category is set up where the morphisms capture the
interplay between the original space and the space of observations. In
order to interpret the relative entropy as a functor they use Lawvere’s
category which consists of a single object and a morphism for every
extended positive real number
[
Law73
]
.

Our contribution is to develop the theory of Baez et al. in the setting
of standard Borel spaces; their
work is carried out with finite sets. While the work of
[
BF14
]

gives a firm conceptual direction, it gives little guidance in the actual
development of the mathematical theory. We had to redevelop the
mathematical framework and find the right analogues for the concepts
appropriate to the finite case.

2.
Background

In this section we review some of the background. We assume that the
reader is familiar with concepts from topology and measure theory as well
as basic category theory. We have found books by Ash
[
Ash72
]
,
Billingsley
[
Bil95
]
and Dudley
[
Dud89
]
to be useful.

We will use letters like
X,Y,Z
for measurable spaces and
capital Greek letters like
\Sigma,\Lambda,\Omega
for
\sigma
-algebras.
We will use
p,q,\ldots
for probability
measures. Given
(X,\Sigma)
and
(Y,\Lambda)
and a measurable function

f:X\to Y
and a probability measure
p
on
(X,\Sigma)
we obtain a measure on

(Y,\Lambda)
by
p\circ f^{-1}
; this is called the
pushforward

measure or the
image
measure.

2.1.
The Giry monad

We denote the category of measurable spaces and measurable functions by

\mathbf{Mes}{}
. We recall the Giry
[
Gir81
]
functor

\Gamma:\mathbf{Mes}{}\rightarrow\mathbf{Mes}{}
which maps each measurable space
X

to the space
\Gamma(X)
of probability measures over
X
. Let

A\in\Sigma
, we define
\mathsf{ev}_{A}:\Gamma(X)\to[0,1]
by

\mathsf{ev}_{A}(p)=p(A)
. We endow
\Gamma(X)
with the smallest

\sigma
-algebra making all the
\mathsf{ev}
’s measurable. A morphism

f:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{Mes}{}
is mapped to
\Gamma(f):\Gamma(X)\to\Gamma(Y)
by

\Gamma(f)(p)=p\circ f^{-1}
. With the following natural
transformations, this endofunctor is a monad: the Giry monad. The natural
transformation
\mathbf{\eta}:I\to\Gamma
is given by
\mathbf{\eta}_{X}(x)=\delta_{x}
, the
Dirac measure concentrated at
x
. The monad multiplication

\mathbf{\mu}:\Gamma^{2}\rightarrow\Gamma
is given by

\forall A\in\mathcal{B}(X),\;\mathbf{\mu}_{X}(p)(A):=\int_{\Gamma(X)}\mathsf{ev}_{A}\;\mathrm{d}p

where
p
is a probability measure in
\Gamma(\Gamma(X))
and

\mathsf{ev}_{A}:\Gamma(X)\to[0,1]
is the measurable function on
\Gamma(X)

defined by
\mathsf{ev}_{A}(p)=p(A)
.

Even if
\mathbf{Mes}{}
is an interesting category in and of itself, the need for
regular conditional probabilities forces us to restrict ourselves to a
subcategory of standard Borel spaces.

2.2.
Standard Borel spaces and disintegration

The Radon-Nikodym theorem is the main tool used to show the existence of
conditional probability distributions, also called Markov kernels, see the
discussion below. It is a very general theorem, but it does not give as
strong regularity features as one might want. A stronger theorem is
needed; this is the so-called
disintegration theorem
. It requires
stronger hypotheses on the space on which the kernels are being defined. A
category of spaces that satisfy these stronger hypotheses is the category
of standard Borel spaces. In order to define standard Borel spaces, we
must first define Polish spaces.

{defi}

A
Polish space
is a separable, completely metrizable topological
space.

{defi}

A
standard Borel space
is a measurable space obtained by forgetting
the topology of a Polish space but retaining its Borel algebra. The
category of standard Borel spaces has measurable functions as morphisms; we
denote it by
{\mathbf{StBor}}
.

We can now state a version of the
disintegration theorem
. The
following is also known as
Rohlin’s disintegration
theorem
.

{thmC}
[
[
Rok49
]
]
Let
(X,p)
and
(Y,q)
be two standard Borel spaces equipped with probability
measures, where
q
is the pushforward measure
q:=p\circ f^{-1}
for
a Borel measurable function
f:X\rightarrow Y
. Then, there exists a

q
-almost everywhere uniquely determined family of probability measures

\{p_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
on
X
such that

(1)

the function
y\mapsto p_{y}(A)
is a Borel-measurable function for
each Borel-measurable set
A\subset X
;

(2)

p_{y}
is a probability measure on
f^{-1}(y)
for
q
-almost all
y\in Y
;

(3)

for every Borel-measurable function
h:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
,

\int_{X}h\;\mathrm{d}p=\int_{Y}\int_{f^{-1}(y)}h\;\mathrm{d}p_{y}\mathrm{d}q.

The objects obtained are often called
regular conditional
probability distributions
.
One can find a crisp categorical formulation of disintegration in
[
CDDG17
, Theorem 1]
.

2.3.
The Kleisli category of
\Gamma
on
{\mathbf{StBor}}

It is well known that the Giry monad on
\mathbf{Mes}{}
restricted to
{\mathbf{StBor}}
admits the same monad structure.
[
Gir81
]

The Kleisli category of
\Gamma
has as objects standard Borel spaces and as
morphisms maps from
X
to
\Gamma(Y)
:
h:X\to(\mathcal{B}_{Y}\to[0,1])
which are
measurable. Here
\mathcal{B}_{Y}
stands for the Borel sets of
Y
and
\Gamma(Y)
has
the
\sigma
-algebra described above. Now we can curry this to write it as

h:X\times\mathcal{B}_{Y}\to[0,1]
or
h(x,U)
where
x
is a point in
X
and
U
is a
Borel set in
Y
. Written this way it is called a Markov kernel and
one can view it as a transition probability function or conditional
probability distribution given
x
. Composition of morphisms
f:X\to Y

and
g:Y\to Z
in the Kleisli category is given by the formula

(g\circ f)(x,V\in\mathcal{B}_{Z})=\int_{Y}g(y,V)\;\mathrm{d}f(x,\cdot).

For an arrow
s:Y\rightarrow\Gamma(X)
in
{\mathbf{StBor}}
, we write
s_{y}
for
s(y)
or, in
kernel form
s(y,\cdot)
. For arrows
t:Z\rightarrow\Gamma(Y)
and

s:Y\rightarrow\Gamma(X)
in
{\mathbf{StBor}}
, we denote their Kleisli composition by

s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t:=\mathbf{\mu}_{X}\circ\Gamma(s)\circ t
. For standard Borel spaces equipped with a
probability measure
p
, we sometimes omit the measure in the notation,

i.e.
we sometimes write
X
instead of
(X,p)
. We say a probability measure
p
is

absolutely continuous
with respect to another measure
q
on the
same measurable space
X
, denoted by
p\ll q
, if for all measurable
sets
B
,
q(B)=0
implies that
p(B)=0
.

We note that absolute continuity is preserved by Kleisli composition; the
proof is straightforward.

Proposition 1
.

Given a standard Borel space
Y
with probability measures
q
and
q^{\prime}
such that

q\ll q^{\prime}
. Then, for arbitrary standard Borel space
X
and morphism
s
from
Y

to
\Gamma(X)
, we have
s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q\ll s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q^{\prime}
.

3.
The categorical setting

In this section, following Baez and Fritz
[
BF14
]
(see also

[
BFL11
]
) we describe the category
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
which
they use for their characterization of entropy on finite spaces. We
then introduce the category
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
which will be the arena for the
generalization to standard Borel spaces.

Before doing so, we define the notion of coherence which will play an
important role in what follows.

{defi}

Given standard Borel spaces
X
and
Y
with probability measure
p
and
q
, respectively, a pair
(f,s)
, with
f:(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
and
s:Y\rightarrow\Gamma(X)
measurable, is
said to be
coherent
1
1

1

Note that a coherent pair
(f,s)
by definition satisfies condition (1) and condition (2) of Theorem (
2.2
) but is not required to satisfy condition (3).
when
f
is measure preserving, i.e.,
q=p\circ f^{-1}
, and
s_{y}

is a probability measure on
f^{-1}(y)

q
-almost everywhere.
2
2

2

Note that
(f,s)
being coherent is equivalent to
\eta_{Y}=\Gamma(f)\circ s
.
If in addition,
p
is absolutely
continuous with respect to
s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q
, then we say that
(f,s)
is

absolutely coherent
.

{defi}

The category
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
has

•

Objects
: Pairs
(X,p)
where
X
is a finite set and
p
a
probability measure on
X
.

•

Morphisms
:
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)
are all coherent pairs
(f,s)
,
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
s:Y\rightarrow\Gamma(X)
.

We compose arrows
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
and

(g,t):(Y,q)\rightarrow(Z,m)
as follows:

(g,t)\circ(f,s):=(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}_{fin}\;t)
where
\;\tilde{\circ}_{fin}\;
is defined
as

(s\;\tilde{\circ}_{fin}\;t)_{z}(x)=\sum_{y\in Y}t_{z}(y)s_{y}(x).

We now leave the finite world for a more general one: the category
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

{defi}

The category
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
has

•

Objects
: Pairs
(X,p)
where
X
is a standard Borel space and
p
a
probability measure on the Borel subsets of
X
.

•

Morphisms
:
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)
are all coherent pairs

(f,s)
,
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
s:Y\rightarrow\Gamma(X)
.

We compose arrows
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
and
(g,t):(Y,q)\rightarrow(Z,m)
as follows:
(g,t)\circ(f,s):=(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
.

Note that the identity arrow on object
(X,p)
is
(id_{X},\eta_{X})
where
id_{X}
is the identity function on
X
. Following the graphical representation from
[
BF14
]
we represent
composition as follows:

One can think of
f
as a measurement process from
X
to
Y
and of
s
as a hypothesis about
X
given an observation in
Y
. We say that a hypothesis
s
is
optimal
3
3

3

For a coherent pair
(f,s)
, asking
s
to be optimal is equivalent to asking that
(f,s)
satisfies condition (3) in Theorem (
2.2
) as will be shown in Lemma (
3
).
if
p=s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q
. We denote by
\mathbf{FP}
the subcategory of
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
consisting of the same objects, but with only those morphisms where the hypothesis is optimal. See
[
BFL11
,
BF14
]
and
[
Lei
]
for a discussion of these ideas in the finite case.

Proposition 2
.

Given coherent pairs the composition is coherent. If, in addition, they are
absolutely coherent, the composition is absolutely coherent.

Proof 3.1
.

We first show that the composition is coherent, i.e.,
\mathbf{\eta}_{Z}=\left(\Gamma(g)\circ\Gamma(f)\right)\circ(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
. It is sufficient to show that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{2}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\mathbf{\eta}_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\eta}_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mu}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{2}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(g)}

Using the hypothesis that
\mathbf{\eta}_{Z}=\Gamma(g)\circ t
and the fact that
\operatorname{Id}=\mu\circ\Gamma(\mathbf{\eta})
, we get that the right-hand square commutes. The triangle commutes since it is the application of
\Gamma
to our hypothesis
\mathbf{\eta}_{Y}=\Gamma(f)\circ s
and the left-hand square commutes because
\mu
is a natural transformation. Therefore, the whole diagram commutes and we have thus shown the composition of coherent morphisms is also coherent.

Next, in addition, assume the pairs
(f,s)
and
(g,t)
are absolutely coherent. We show

p\ll(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)
. By hypotheses,
p\ll s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q
and
q\ll t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m
. Using Proposition
1
on
q\ll t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m
, we get
s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q\ll s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m
. By transitivity of
\ll
, we conclude
p\ll(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)
.

We end this section by defining one more category; this one is due to
Lawvere
[
Law73
]
. It is just the set
[0,\infty]
but endowed with categorical
structure. This allows numerical values associated with morphisms to
be regarded as functors.

{defi}

The category
[0,\infty]
has

•

Objects
: One single object:
\bullet
.

•

Morphisms
: For each element
r\in[0,\infty]
, one arrow

r:\bullet\rightarrow\bullet
.

Arrow composition is defined as addition in
[0,\infty]
. Consequently,
0
is the identity arrow.

This is a remarkable category with monoidal closed structure and many other
interesting properties.

4.
Relative entropy functor

We recapitulate the definition of the relative entropy functor on
{\mathbf{FinStat}}

from Baez and Fritz
[
BF14
]
and then extend it to
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

{defi}

The relative entropy functor
RE_{fin}
is defined from
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
to
\mathtt{[0,\infty]}
as follows:

•

On Objects
: It maps every object
(X,p)
to
\bullet
.

•

On Morphisms
: It maps a morphism
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
to
S_{fin}(p,s\;\tilde{\circ}_{fin}\;q)
, where

S_{fin}(p,s\;\tilde{\circ}_{fin}\;q):=\sum_{x\in X}p(x)\ln\left(\frac{p(x)}{(s\;\tilde{\circ}_{fin}\;q)(x)}\right).

The convention from now on will be that
\infty\cdot c=c\cdot\infty=\infty
for
0<c\leq\infty
and
\infty\cdot 0=0\cdot\infty=0
. We extend

RE_{fin}
from
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
to
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

{defi}

The relative entropy functor
RE
is defined from
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
to
\mathtt{[0,\infty]}
as follows:

•

On Objects
: It maps every object
(X,p)
to
\bullet
.

•

On Morphisms
: Given a coherent morphism
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
, if
(f,s)
is absolutely coherent, then

RE((f,s))=S(p,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)
, where

S(p,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q):=\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p,

otherwise it is defined as
RE((f,s))=\infty
.

This quantity is also known as the
Kullback-Leibler divergence
.

We could have defined our category to have only absolutely coherent
morphisms but it would make the comparison with the finite case more
awkward as the finite case does not assume the morphisms to be absolutely coherent. The present definition leads to slightly awkward proofs where we
have to consider absolutely coherent pairs and ordinary coherent pairs
separately.

Clearly,
RE
restricts to
RE_{fin}
on
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
. If
(f,s)
is
absolutely coherent, then
p
is absolutely continuous with respect to

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)
and the Radon-Nikodym derivative is defined. The relative
entropy is always non-negative
[
KL51
]
; this is an easy
consequence of Jensen’s inequality. This shows that
RE
is defined
everywhere in
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\!\!\bigg).

In order to do so, we will need the following two lemmas.

Lemma 3
.

Given an arrow
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
in
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
. Let
\{(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
be a disintegration
of
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)
along
f
, then

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}=s_{y}\text{ $q$-almost everywhere.}

We just have to show that
\{s_{y}\}_{y\in Y}
satisfies the three
properties implied by the disintegration theorem. We prove the third
one; the first two being obvious.

2.2
(iii)
: For every Borel-measurable function
h:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
,

\int_{X}h\;\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)=\int_{y\in Y}\int_{f^{-1}(y)}h\;\mathrm{d}s_{y}\;\mathrm{d}q.

Proof 4.1
.

Let’s assume as a special case that
h
is the indicator function for a
measurable set
E\subset X
. Then, we have

\int_{X}h\;\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)=\int_{E}\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)=(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(E)=\int_{y\in Y}s_{y}(E)\;\mathrm{d}q=\int_{y\in Y}\int_{f^{-1}(y)}h\;\mathrm{d}s_{y}\;\mathrm{d}q.

We have shown that it is true for any indicator function. By linearity, it
is true for any simple function and then, by the monotone convergence
theorem, it is true for all Borel-measurable functions
h:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
.

Lemma 4
.

The relative entropy is preserved under
pre-composition by optimal hypotheses, i.e., for any
(g,t):(Y,q)\rightarrow(Z,m)
and
(f,s):(X,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)\rightarrow(Y,q)
,
we have

RE\bigg(\!\!\!\hbox to114.13pt{\vbox to46.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.0638pt\lower-23.3921pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Proof 4.2
.

Case I :
(g,t)
is absolutely coherent.
Since
(g,t)
is
absolutely coherent, so is
(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
by Proposition

1
. Hence, to show
RE(g,t)=RE(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
is to show

\int_{Y}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}q=\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q).

Because
f
is measure preserving, it is sufficient to show that the following functions on
X

\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\circ f=\frac{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\text{ $s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m$-almost everywhere.}

By the Radon-Nikodym theorem, itx is sufficient to show that for any
E\subset X
measurable set, we have

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(E)=\int_{E}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\circ f\;\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m).

The following calculation establishes the above.

\displaystyle\int_{E}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\circ f\;\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Y}\left(\int_{x\in f^{-1}(y)\cap E}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\circ f\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)_{y}\right)\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

(1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Y}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}(y)\left(\int_{f^{-1}(y)\cap E}\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)_{y}\right)\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

(2)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Y}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}(y)\left(\int_{f^{-1}(y)\cap E}\mathrm{d}s_{y}\right)\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

(3)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Y}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}(y)s_{y}(E\cap f^{-1}(y))\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Y}\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}(y)s_{y}(E)\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

(4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Y}s_{y}(E)\;\mathrm{d}q

(5)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(E)

(6)

We get (
1
) by applying the
disintegration theorem to
f:\left(X,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m\right)\rightarrow(Y,t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)
. The equation
(
2
) follows by using the fact that

\frac{\mathrm{d}q}{\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\circ f
is constant on
f^{-1}(y)
for every
y
.
To obtain (
3
) we apply Lemma
3
. To show
(
4
) we use the fact that
s_{y}
is a probability measure on

f^{-1}(y)
. We get (
5
) by the definition of the Radon-Nikodym derivative and
we finally establish (
6
) by the definition of Kleisli composition.

Case II :
(g,t)
is not absolutely coherent.
We have
RE((g,t))=\infty
. We show that
(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
is not absolutely coherent,
i.e.,
s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m
.

Since, by hypothesis,
q\ll t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m
doesn’t hold,
there exists a measurable set
B\subset Y
such that
(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(B)=0
but
q(B)>0
. We argue that
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(f^{-1}(B))=0
and
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(f^{-1}(B))>0
.
On one hand, we have

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(f^{-1}(B))=\int_{B}s_{y}(f^{-1}(B))\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)\leq(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(B)=0.

But on the other hand, since
f
is a measure preserving map from
(X,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)
to

(Y,q)
, we have
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(f^{-1}(B))=q(B)>0
.

Therefore,

RE((g,t))=\infty=RE((g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)).

Theorem 5

(Functoriality)
.

Given arrows
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
and
(g,t):(Y,q)\rightarrow(Z,m)
, we have

RE\left((g,t)\circ(f,s)\right)=RE((g,t))+RE((f,s)).

Proof 4.3
.

Note that by definition,
RE\left((g,t)\circ(f,s)\right)=RE\left((g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)\right)
.

Case I :
(f,s)
and
(g,t)
are absolutely coherent.

By Proposition
2
, we have that
(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
is
absolutely coherent.

\displaystyle RE\left((g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}\frac{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p

(7)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p+\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle=RE((f,s))+\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle=RE((f,s))+RE((g,t))

(8)

We get (
7
) by the chain rule for Radon-Nikodym derivatives and (
8
)
by applying Lemma
4
.

Case II :
(g,t)
is not absolutely coherent.

We argue that
(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
is not absolutely coherent.
By hypothesis,
q\ll t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m
doesn’t hold, so there is a measurable set
B\subset Y
such that
(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(B)=0
and
q(B)>0
. We show that
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)\left(f^{-1}(B)\right)=0
and
p(f^{-1}(B))>0
. On one hand, we have

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)\left(f^{-1}(B)\right)=\int_{B}s_{y}\left(f^{-1}(B)\right)\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)\leq(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(B)=0,

but on the other hand, we have
p(f^{-1}(B))=q(B)>0
.
Therefore

RE\left((g,t)\circ(f,s)\right)=\infty=RE((g,t))+RE((f,s)).

Case III :
(f,s)
is not absolutely coherent.

This case is not analogous to the previous case since the existence of a measurable set
A\subset X
such that
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(A)=0
and
p(A)>0
is surprisingly not enough to conclude that
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(A)=0
.

By the hypothesis of
(f,s)
not being absolutely coherent,
p\ll s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q
doesn’t hold, so there is a measurable set
A\subset X
such that

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(A)=0
and
p(A)>0
.

We partition
A
into

A_{\epsilon}:=\{x\in A\;|\;s_{f(x)}(A)>0\}\text{ and }A_{0}:=\{x\in A\;|\;s_{f(x)}(A)=0\}

and we partition
Y
into

B_{\epsilon}:=\{y\in Y\;|\;s_{y}(A)>0\}\text{ and }B_{0}:=\{y\in Y\;|\;s_{y}(A)=0\}.

We argue that
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(A_{0})=0
and
p(A_{0})>0
.

Since
A_{0}\subset f^{-1}(B_{0})
,
f^{-1}(B_{\epsilon})
is disjoint from
A_{0}
, so for all
y\in B_{\epsilon}
we have
s_{y}(A_{0})=0
because their support is disjoint from
A_{0}
. On one hand, we thus have

\displaystyle(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)(A_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}s_{y}(A_{0})\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{0}}s_{y}(A_{0})\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)+\int_{B_{\epsilon}}s_{y}(A_{0})\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{0}}s_{y}(A_{0})\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{0}}s_{y}(A)\;\mathrm{d}(t\;\tilde{\circ}\;m)

\displaystyle=0.

On the other hand, since we have
p(A_{0})+p(A_{\epsilon})=p(A)>0
and

A_{\epsilon}\subset f^{-1}(B_{\epsilon})
, it suffices to show

p(f^{-1}(B_{\epsilon}))=0
to conclude
p(A_{0})>0
.

By hypothesis, we have

(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(A)=\int_{B_{0}}s_{y}(A)\;\mathrm{d}q+\int_{B_{\epsilon}}s_{y}(A)\;\mathrm{d}q=0,

so
q(B_{\epsilon})=0
and because
f
is measure preserving, we have
p(f^{-1}(B_{\epsilon}))=q(B_{\epsilon})=0
as desired.

So
(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;t)
is not absolutely coherent, hence

RE\left((g,t)\circ(f,s)\right)=\infty=RE((g,t))+RE((f,s)).

This completes the proof of this case.

We have thus shown that
RE
is a well-defined functor from
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
to
[0,\infty]
.

4.1.
Convex linearity

We show below that the relative entropy functor satisfies a convex
linearity property. In
[
BF14
]
convexity looks familiar; here since
we are performing “large” sums we have to express it as an integral.
First we define a localized version of the relative entropy.

Note that Lemma
3
says that
s_{y}=(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}

q
-almost everywhere. Thus, in the following there is no
notational clash between the kernel
s_{y}
and
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}
, the later
being the disintegration of
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)
along
f
.

Given an arrow
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
in
{\mathbf{StBor}}
and a point
y\in Y
, we denote by
(f,s)_{y}
, the morphism
(f,s)
restricted to the pair
of standard Borel spaces
f^{-1}(y)
and
\{y\}
. Explicitly,

(f,s)_{y}:=(\left.f\right|_{f^{-1}(y)},s_{y}):(f^{-1}(y),p_{y})\longrightarrow(\{y\},\delta_{y}),

where
\delta_{y}
is the one and only probability measure on
\{y\}
.

{defi}

A functor
F
from
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
to
\mathtt{[0,\infty]}
is
convex linear
if for every
arrow
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
, we have

F\left((f,s)\right)=\int_{Y}F\left((f,s)_{y}\right)\;\mathrm{d}q.

We will sometimes refer to the relative entropy of
(f,s)_{y}
as the
local relative entropy of
(f,s)
at
y
. Before proving that RE is convex linear, we first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6
.

Given

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,p^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
f
is a measurable map preserving the measure of both Borel probability measures
p
and
p^{\prime}
. If
p\ll p^{\prime}
, then
\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{y}}{\mathrm{d}p_{y}^{\prime}}
is defined for
q
-almost every
y
and

\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{y}}{\mathrm{d}p_{y}^{\prime}}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}}(x)\;\text{ $p^{\prime}$-almost everywhere.}

Proof 4.4
.

For an arbitrary measurable function
h:X\rightarrow[0,\infty]
, by first applying the Radon-Nikodym theorem and then the
disintegration theorem on the measurable
function
h\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}}
, we get

\int_{X}h\;\mathrm{d}p=\int_{X}h\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}}\;\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}=\int_{Y}\int_{f^{-1}(y)}h\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}}\;\mathrm{d}p_{y}^{\prime}\;\mathrm{d}q.

Hence, for
q
-almost every
y
, we must have

\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{y}}{\mathrm{d}p_{y}^{\prime}}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}}(x)

p^{\prime}
-almost everywhere.

Theorem 7

(Convex Linearity)
.

The functor RE is convex linear, i.e., for every arrow
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
, we have

RE((f,s))=\int_{Y}RE\left((f,s)_{y}\right)\;\mathrm{d}q.

Proof 4.5
.

Case I :
(f,s)
is absolutely coherent.

We have

\displaystyle RE((f,s))

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}\right)\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}\int_{f^{-1}(y)}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)}\right)\mathrm{d}p_{y}\;\mathrm{d}q

(9)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}\int_{f^{-1}(y)}\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{y}}{\mathrm{d}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}}\right)\;\mathrm{d}p_{y}\;\mathrm{d}q

(10)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}RE((f,s)_{y})\;\mathrm{d}q.

We get (
9
) by the disintegration theorem and (
10
)
by applying Lemma
6
.

Case II :
(f,s)
is not absolutely coherent.

By the hypothesis of
(f,s)
not being absolutely coherent, there is a measurable set
A\subset X
such that
(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(A)=0
and
p(A)>0
. Applying lemma
3
, on one hand we have

\int_{Y}(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}(A)\;\mathrm{d}q=\int_{Y}s_{y}(A)\;\mathrm{d}q=(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)(A)=0,

but on the other
hand we have

\int_{Y}p_{y}(A)\;\mathrm{d}q=p(A)>0.

Hence, the subset of
Y
on which

p_{y}\ll(s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)_{y}
doesn’t hold contains a set of measure strictly
greater than
0
. Therefore,

RE((f,s))=\infty=\int_{Y}RE\left((f,s)_{y}\right)\;\mathrm{d}q.

4.2.
Lower-semi-continuity

Recall that a sequence of probability measures
p_{n}
converges strongly to
p
, denoted by
p_{n}\rightarrow p
, if for all measurable set
E
, one has
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}(E)=p(E)
.

The singleton set equipped with the trivial measure, which we will denote by
(1,\delta)
, is a weakly terminal object of
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
, it is weakly terminal in the sense that for every
(X,p)
there exist a non-unique arrow
(f,s):(X,p)\to(1,\delta)
in
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

{defi}

A functor
F
from
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
to
\mathtt{[0,\infty]}
is
lower semi-continuous
if for every arrow
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(1,\delta)
, whenever
p_{n}\rightarrow p
and
s_{n}\rightarrow s
, then

F\bigg(\!\!\!\hbox to108.66pt{\vbox to46.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.32805pt\lower-24.79921pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Recall that in
[
BF14
]
, lower semicontinuity was defined on
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
as the following.

{defi}

[Baez and Fritz]
A functor
F:\mbox{${\mathbf{FinStat}}$}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is
lower semicontinuous
if for any sequence of morphisms
(f,s_{i}):(X,p_{i})\rightarrow(Y,q_{i})
that converges
4
4

4

Where convergence is just pointwise convergence.
to a morphism
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
, we have

F(f,s)\leq\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}F(f,s_{i}).

Recalling that
\mathbf{FP}
stands for the subcategory of
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
consisting of the same objects, but with only those morphisms where the hypothesis is optimal. We claim that a lower semi-continuous (as defined in
Definition
4.2
) functor
F
that vanishes on
\mathbf{FP}
restricts to a lower semi-continuous functor on
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
(as defined in
Definition
4.2
). To see this, note that, given a sequence of morphisms
(f,s_{i}):(X,p_{i})\rightarrow(Y,q_{i})
that converges pointwise to a morphism
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(Y,q)
, we can recover

F(f,s)\leq\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}F(f,s_{i})

from

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,p_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Y,q_{i}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,\delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}

and

\displaystyle F(f,s)

\displaystyle=F(f,s)+\overbrace{F(g,q)}^{0}=F(g\circ f,s\;\tilde{\circ}\;q)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}F(g\circ f,s_{i}\;\tilde{\circ}\;q_{i})=\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}F(f,s_{i})+\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}\underbrace{F(g,q_{i})}_{0}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}F(f,s_{i}).

Note that, on finite sets, converging pointwise is equivalent to strong convergence.

Theorem 8

(Lower semi-continuity)
.

The functor
RE
is lower semi-continuous.

.

as desired.

5.
Uniqueness

We now show that the relative entropy is, up to a multiplicative constant,
the unique functor satisfying the conditions established so far. We first
prove a crucial lemma.

Lemma 9
.

Let
X
be a Borel space equipped with probability measures
p
and
q
,
if
p\ll q
, then we can find a sequence of simple functions
p_{n}^{*}
on

X
such that for the sequence of probability measures

p_{n}(E):=\int_{E}p_{n}^{*}\;\mathrm{d}q
, we have that
p_{n}
and
p
agree on
the elements of the partition on
X
induced by
p_{n}^{*}
and moreover,

p_{n}\rightarrow p
strongly.

Proof 5.1
.

We write
I_{n,k}
for the interval
[k2^{-n},(k+1)2^{-n})
and

I_{n,\leq}
for the interval
[n,\infty)
. Denote by
K_{n}
the index
set
\{0,1,\ldots,n2^{n}-1,\leq\}
of
k
. We fix a version

\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}
of the Radon-Nikodym such that

\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}<\infty
everywhere. We define a family of
partitions and a family of simple functions as follows:

X_{n,k}:=\left\{x^{\prime}\in X\;|\;\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x^{\prime})\in I_{n,k}\right\},\qquad p_{n}^{*}(x):=\frac{p\left(X_{n,k}\right)}{q\left(X_{n,k}\right)}\text{ on }x\in X_{n,k}.

Every function induces a partition on the domain; if moreover the function
is simple, the induced partition is finite.

We first note that
p_{n}
and
p
agree on the elements of the partition induced by
p_{n}^{*}
:

p_{n}(X_{n,k})=\int_{X_{n,k}}p_{n}^{*}\;\mathrm{d}q=\int_{X_{n,k}}\frac{p\left(X_{n,k}\right)}{q\left(X_{n,k}\right)}\;\mathrm{d}q=\frac{p\left(X_{n,k}\right)}{q\left(X_{n,k}\right)}q(X_{n,k})=p(X_{n,k}).

Next, we prove the strong convergence of
p_{n}\rightarrow p
. We first show
p_{n}^{*}\rightarrow\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}
pointwise. Let
x\in X
. Pick
N
large enough such that
\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)\leq N
. For a fixed integer
n\geq N
, there is exactly one
k_{n}
for which
x\in X_{n,k_{n}}
. On the one hand, we have
k_{n}2^{-n}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)\leq(k_{n}+1)2^{-n}
on
X_{n,k_{n}}
. But on the other hand, by integrating over
X_{n,k_{n}}
and dividing everything by
q(X_{n,k_{n}})
, we also have
k_{n}2^{-n}\leq\frac{p\left(X_{n,k_{n}}\right)}{q\left(X_{n,k_{n}}\right)}\leq(k_{n}+1)2^{-n}
on
X_{n,k_{n}}
. We thus get pointwise convergence since we have

\left|p_{n}^{*}(x)-\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)\right|=\left|\frac{p\left(X_{n,k_{n}}\right)}{q\left(X_{n,k_{n}}\right)}-\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)\right|\leq 2^{-n}\text{ for any }n\geq N.

From the above inequality and the choice of
N
, we note the following

p_{n}^{*}(x)\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)+2^{-n}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)+1,\quad\text{for $x$ with $\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)<n$},

p_{n}^{*}(x)=p(X_{n,\leq})\leq 1\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)+1,\quad\text{ for $x$ with $\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)\geq n$}.

So for all
n
, we can bound
p_{n}^{*}(x)
everywhere by the integrable function
g(x):=\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}(x)+1
. Given a measurable set
E\subset X
, we can thus apply Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. We get

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}(E)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{E}p_{n}^{*}\;\mathrm{d}q=\int_{E}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}^{*}\;\mathrm{d}q=\int_{E}\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q}\;\mathrm{d}q=p(E).

Before proving uniqueness, we recall the main theorem of Baez and Fritz
[
BF14
]
on
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
.

Theorem 10
.

Suppose that a functor

F:\mbox{${\mathbf{FinStat}}$}{}\rightarrow[0,\infty]

is lower
semicontinuous, convex linear and vanishes on
\mathbf{FP}
. Then for some
0\leq c\leq\infty
we have
F(f,s)=cRE_{fin}(f,s)
for all morphisms
(f,s)
in

{\mathbf{FinStat}}
.

We are now ready to extend this characterization to
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

Theorem 11
.

Suppose that a functor

F:\mbox{${\mathbf{SbStat}}$}\rightarrow[0,\infty]

is lower semicontinuous, convex linear and vanishes on
\mathbf{FP}
.
Then for some
0\leq c\leq\infty
we have
F(f,s)=cRE(f,s)
for all morphisms.

Proof 5.2
.

Since
F
satisfies all the above properties on
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
, we can apply Theorem
10
in order to establish that
F=cRE_{fin}=cRE

for all morphisms in the subcategory
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
. We show that
F
extends
uniquely to
cRE
on all morphisms in
{\mathbf{SbStat}}
.

By convex linearity of
F
, for an arbitrary morphism
(f,s)
from
(X,p)
to
(Y,q)
, we have

F((f,s))=\int_{Y}F((f,s)_{y})\;\mathrm{d}q,

so
F

is totally described by its local relative entropies. It is thus sufficient to
show
F=cRE
on an arbitrary morphism
(f,s):(X,p)\rightarrow(1,\delta)
. The case where
p
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
s
is straightforward, so let us assume
p\ll s
.

We apply Lemma
9
with
p
and
s
to get the family of simple functions
p_{n}^{*}
and the corresponding family of partitions
\{X_{n,k}\}
. We define
\pi_{n}
as the function that maps
x\in X_{n,k^{\prime}}
to the element
X_{n,k^{\prime}}\in\{X_{n,k}\}_{k\in K_{n}}
. Denote by
s_{\pi_{n}}
the disintegration of
s
along
\pi_{n}
and by
s_{n}
the corresponding marginal. Note that since
p_{n}
and
p
agree on every
X_{n,k}
,
p_{n}
is indeed the push-forward of
p
along
\pi_{n}
. Presented as diagrams, we have

From the above diagram and the hypothesis that
F
is a functor, we have the following inequality

\displaystyle F((f_{n},s_{n}))\leq F((f,s))\text{, for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$}.

(11)

Note that, on the one hand the disintegration of
p_{n}
along
\pi_{n}
at the point
X_{n,k^{\prime}}\in\{X_{n,k}\}
is given by
p_{n,\pi}:=p_{n}(\cdot)/p_{n}(X_{n,k^{\prime}})
, but on the other hand, for any measurable set
E\subset X
, we also have

\sum_{k\in K_{n}}\left(\int_{X_{n,k}}\mathbbm{1}_{E}\;\mathrm{d}p_{n,\pi}\right)s_{n}(X_{n,k})=\sum_{k\in K_{n}}\left(\frac{p_{n}(E\cap X_{n,k})}{p_{n}(X_{n,k})}\right)s_{n}(X_{n,k})

=\sum_{k\in K_{n}}\left(\frac{s(E\cap X_{n,k})}{s(X_{n,k})}\right)s_{n}(X_{n,k})=\sum_{k\in K_{n}}s(E\cap X_{n,k})=s(E).

This means that
p_{n,\pi}
is the disintegration of
s
along
\pi_{n}
. Presented as diagrams, where we use
f^{p_{n}}
instead of
f
to indicate that the arrow leaves from the object
(X,p_{n})
as opposed to
(X,p)
, we have

But since
F
vanishes on
\mathbf{FP}
, we have
F\left((\pi_{n},p_{n,\pi})\right)=0
. Combined with the fact that
F
is a functor, we get

\displaystyle F\left((f^{p_{n}},s)\right)=F\left((\pi_{n},p_{n,\pi})\right)+F\left((f_{n},s_{n})\right)=F\left((f_{n},s_{n})\right).

(12)

By Lemma
9
, we know that
p_{n}\rightarrow p
, in terms of our diagrams we have

Hence, combining (
12
) with the lower semicontinuity of
F
, we also have the inequality

\displaystyle F((f,s))\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}F((f^{p_{n}},s))=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}F((f_{n},s_{n})).

(13)

Since
(f_{n},s_{n})
is in
{\mathbf{FinStat}}
, we must have
F((f_{n},s_{n}))=cRE((f_{n},s_{n}))
.
Thus, combining (
11
) and (
13
), we get that
F((f,s))
must satisfy

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}cRE((f_{n},s_{n}))\leq F((f,s))\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}cRE((f_{n},s_{n})),

but so does
cRE((f,s))
. We also have

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}cRE((f_{n},s_{n}))\leq cRE((f,s))\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}cRE((f_{n},s_{n})).

Therefore
F((f,s))=cRE((f,s))
, as desired.

6.
Conclusions and Further Directions

As promised, we have given a categorial characterization of relative
entropy on standard Borel spaces. This greatly broadens the scope of the original
work by Baez et al.
[
BFL11
,
BF14
]
. However, the main
motivation is to study the role of entropy arguments in machine learning.
These appear in various ad-hoc ways in machine learning but with the appearance of
the recent work by Danos and his
co-workers
[
DG15
,
CDDG17
,
DDG16
]
we feel that we have the
prospect of a mathematically well-defined framework on which to understand
Bayesian inversion and its interplay with entropy. The most recent paper
in this series
[
CDDG17
]
adopts a point-free approach introduced
in
[
CDPP09
,
CDPP14
]
. It would be interesting to extend our
definitions to a point-free situation.
